## P4 - Unpinned `git clone` of `clab-labs/iouyap-go` in the `iouyap` builder stage

**Location:** [cisco/iol/docker/Dockerfile:1-6](https://github.com/srl-labs/vrnetlab/blob/1b19ec5484a2a544b0f4fdec74cbfa2e5479e34c/cisco/iol/docker/Dockerfile#L1-L6)

**Problem:** The `iouyap` builder stage runs `RUN git clone https://github.com/clab-labs/iouyap-go /src` and builds the working tree with `go build` without pinning a tag, branch, or commit. Every image build compiles whatever is the default-branch tip of an external repository at that moment. This is the only `git clone`-at-build-time pattern in the repository &#40;all `Dockerfile`s were checked&#41;, so there is no established precedent for it.

**Trigger:** Any rebuild of the `cisco_iol` image, including the CI pipeline in [`.gitlab-ci.yml`](https://github.com/srl-labs/vrnetlab/blob/1b19ec5484a2a544b0f4fdec74cbfa2e5479e34c/.gitlab-ci.yml#L1-L1) &#40;`make` -&#62; `docker build`&#41;, pulls the current state of the external repo. A push to `clab-labs/iouyap-go` silently changes the resulting `/usr/bin/iouyap` binary without any change to this repository.

**Impact:** Image builds are not reproducible&#58; two builds of the same commit of this repository can produce different images, and the supervisor binary version is recorded nowhere, which complicates debugging IOL networking regressions. This is an optional improvement rather than a defect&#58; the removed base configuration &#40;`deb [trusted=yes] https://netdevops.fury.site/apt/` with an unpinned `iouyap` apt package&#41; was strictly worse from a supply-chain standpoint, so the PR is already a net improvement.

**Suggested correction:** Pin the source, for example `RUN git clone https://github.com/clab-labs/iouyap-go /src && git -C /src checkout <commit-or-tag>`, ideally exposing the ref or commit SHA as an `ARG` with a default. Optionally document the pinned `iouyap-go` version in [`cisco/iol/README.md`](https://github.com/srl-labs/vrnetlab/blob/1b19ec5484a2a544b0f4fdec74cbfa2e5479e34c/cisco/iol/README.md#L1-L1) so users know which supervisor version ships in the image.


## Coverage

- Snapshot is incomplete per manifest&#58; only `cisco/n9kv/docker/OVMF.fd` and `cisco/xrv9k/docker/OVMF.fd` were skipped &#40;binary size limit&#41;; both are unrelated to the two changed paths.
- The repository review rules referenced by the task &#40;`.cursor/rules/karpathy-guidelines.mdc` and `.cursor/rules/ponytail.mdc`&#41; were unavailable, and `standards.json` is empty, so no repository-specific rules were applied.
- Source-only review&#58; the image was not built and no tests were executed; all conclusions come from static inspection of the snapshot.
- The external `clab-labs/iouyap-go` repository is not part of the snapshot, so the CLI contract of `iouyap 513 -- <command>` is unverified. Four runtime behavior changes remain unconfirmed assumptions consistent with a supervisor rewrite&#58; &#40;1&#41; whether `iouyap-go` derives the ethernet slot count previously passed via `-e $num_slots`, given that the `CLAB_IOL_NUM_SLOTS` fallback computation was removed and containerlab &#40;external repo&#41; is the likely setter of that variable; &#40;2&#41; whether `iouyap-go` performs the removed `ip addr flush dev eth0` cleanup; &#40;3&#41; whether the removed `sleep 5` delay is replaced by interface readiness handling inside `iouyap-go`; and &#40;4&#41; whether `iouyap-go` forwards `docker stop` SIGTERM to the child `iol.bin` now that it is PID 1 instead of `iol.bin`. A maintainer with access to the `iouyap-go` source should confirm these.
- The final image still installs `iproute2`, `net-tools`, `curl`, `gnupg`, and `sudo`, which the new three-line entrypoint no longer uses in this repository; `iouyap-go` or `iol.bin` may still shell out to some of them, so this was left as a note rather than a finding.
- No automated CI or test coverage exists for the IOL image path; the smallest useful validation is a manual containerlab deployment of an IOL topology, including a stop/restart cycle, to exercise the new PID 1 supervisor behavior.
- Both changed paths &#40;`cisco/iol/docker/Dockerfile` and [`cisco/iol/docker/entrypoint.sh`](https://github.com/srl-labs/vrnetlab/blob/1b19ec5484a2a544b0f4fdec74cbfa2e5479e34c/cisco/iol/docker/entrypoint.sh#L1-L1)&#41; were fully reviewed, along with their callers &#40;`cisco/iol/Makefile`, [`makefile.include`](https://github.com/srl-labs/vrnetlab/blob/1b19ec5484a2a544b0f4fdec74cbfa2e5479e34c/makefile.include#L1-L1), [`makefile-install.include`](https://github.com/srl-labs/vrnetlab/blob/1b19ec5484a2a544b0f4fdec74cbfa2e5479e34c/makefile-install.include#L1-L1), [`.gitlab-ci.yml`](https://github.com/srl-labs/vrnetlab/blob/1b19ec5484a2a544b0f4fdec74cbfa2e5479e34c/.gitlab-ci.yml#L1-L1)&#41; and docs &#40;[`cisco/iol/README.md`](https://github.com/srl-labs/vrnetlab/blob/1b19ec5484a2a544b0f4fdec74cbfa2e5479e34c/cisco/iol/README.md#L1-L1)&#41;.

<content>
1: #!/bin/bash
2: 
3: echo "Launching IOL with PID" $IOL_PID
4: 
5: # Clear ip addressing on eth0 (it 'belongs' to IOL now)
6: ip addr flush dev eth0
7: ip -6 addr flush dev eth0
8: 
9: echo "Flushed eth0 addresses"
10: 
11: sleep 5
12: 
13: # Run IOUYAP
14: exec /usr/bin/iouyap 513 -q &
15: 
16: # Use the declared slot count when provided, otherwise get the highest numbered eth interface
17: if [ -n "$CLAB_IOL_NUM_SLOTS" ]; then
18:     num_slots=$CLAB_IOL_NUM_SLOTS
19: else
20:     max_eth=$(ls /sys/class/net | grep eth | grep -o -E '[0-9]+' | sort -n | tail -1)
21:     num_slots=$(( (max_eth + 4) / 4 ))
22: fi
23: 
24: # Start IOL
25: exec /iol/iol.bin $IOL_PID -e $num_slots -s 0 -c config.txt -n 1024

(End of file - total 25 lines)
</content>

Output

<path>/source/head/cisco/iol/Makefile</path>
<type>file</type>
<content>
1: VENDOR=Cisco
2: NAME=IOL
3: IMAGE_FORMAT=bin
4: IMAGE_GLOB=*.bin *.tar.gz
5: DONT_COPY_COMMON=1
6: 
7: # match versions like:
8: # cisco_iol-17.12.01.bin      -> 17.12.01      loose binary (CML <= 2.9 refplat)
9: # cisco_iol-L2-17.12.01.bin   -> L2-17.12.01
10: # iol-xe-17-18-02.tar.gz      -> 17.18.02      container archive (CML >= 2.10)
11: # ioll2-xe-17-18-02.tar.gz    -> L2-17.18.02
12: VERSION=$(shell case '$(IMAGE)' in \
13: 	(ioll2-xe-*.tar.gz) echo '$(IMAGE)' | sed -e 's/^ioll2-xe-//' -e 's/\.tar\.gz$$//' -e 's/-/./g' -e 's/^/L2-/' ;; \
14: 	(iol-xe-*.tar.gz) echo '$(IMAGE)' | sed -e 's/^iol-xe-//' -e 's/\.tar\.gz$$//' -e 's/-/./g' ;; \
15: 	(*) echo '$(IMAGE)' | sed -e 's/cisco_iol-\(.*\)\.bin/\1/' ;; \
16: 	esac)
17: 
18: -include ../../makefile-sanity.include
19: -include ../../makefile.include
20: 
21: # From CML 2.10 onwards IOL is shipped as an OCI/docker image archive instead of
22: # a loose binary. The IOL ELF sits inside one of the layer blobs, so peel it out
23: # on the fly rather than making the user hunt for it by hand.
24: #
25: # The archive is two tars deep:
26: #
27: #   iol-xe-17-18-02.tar.gz
28: #   |-- manifest.json         names the config + layer blobs, layers bottom-first
29: #   `-- blobs/sha256/
30: #       |-- 483954...         config JSON
31: #       |-- 9f410b...         layer 0, itself a tar: libc, iol-runner, .iourc
32: #       `-- ac6972...         layer 1, itself a tar: ...plus the .iol binary
33: #
34: # So: read manifest.json for the blob paths, then probe each blob (stream it out
35: # of the outer tar and list it with an inner tar) for a member ending in .iol.
36: 
37: docker-build-image-copy:
38: 	@case '$(IMAGE)' in \
39: 	*.tar.gz) \
40: 		echo "--> $(IMAGE) is a container archive, extracting IOL binary"; \
41: 		blob=; member=; \
42: 		for b in $$(tar -xzOf '$(IMAGE)' manifest.json | grep -o 'blobs/sha256/[0-9a-f]\{64\}'); do \
43: 			m=$$(tar -xzOf '$(IMAGE)' "$$b" 2>/dev/null | tar -tvf - 2>/dev/null \
44: 				| awk '/^-.*\.iol$$/ { print $$NF }' | tail -1); \
45: 			if [ -n "$$m" ]; then blob=$$b; member=$$m; fi; \
46: 		done; \
47: 		if [ -z "$$member" ]; then \
48: 			echo "ERROR: no IOL binary (*.iol) found in any layer of $(IMAGE)"; exit 1; \
49: 		fi; \
50: 		echo "--> Found $$member in $$blob"; \
51: 		tar -xzOf '$(IMAGE)' "$$blob" | tar -xOf - "$$member" > docker/iol.bin; \
52: 		[ -s docker/iol.bin ] || { echo "ERROR: extracted IOL binary is empty"; exit 1; }; \
53: 		;; \
54: 	*) cp $(IMAGE)* docker/ ;; \
55: 	esac

Output

<path>/source/head/cisco/iol/README.md</path>
<type>file</type>
<content>
1: # Cisco IOL (IOS on Linux)
2: 
3: This is the containerlab/vrnetlab image for Cisco IOL (IOS On Linux). You can find the binary image as part of the CML image, with this [blog](https://marcstech.blog/archives/add-cisco-iol-containerlab-macos/) explaining the process.
4: 
5: Compared to other Cisco images, IOL runs very lightly: it executes purely as a binary and needs no virtualisation layer.
6: 
7: There are two types of IOL you can obtain:
8: 
9: - IOL, meant for Layer 3 operation as a router.
10: - IOL-L2, meant to act as a L2/L2+ switch.
11: 
12: ## Building the image
13: 
14: Copy your IOL image into this directory, named as below, then run `make` or `make docker-image`.
15: 
16: | From                       | File                                  | Name it as                  | Tag           |
17: | -------------------------- | ------------------------------------- | --------------------------- | ------------- |
18: | CML 2.10+ (IOL)            | `iol-xe-17-18-02.tar.gz`              | keep as-is                  | `17.18.02`    |
19: | CML 2.10+ (IOL-L2)         | `ioll2-xe-17-18-02.tar.gz`            | keep as-is                  | `L2-17.18.02` |
20: | CML 2.9 and older (IOL)    | `x86_64_crb_linux-adventerprisek9-ms` | `cisco_iol-17.12.01.bin`    | `17.12.01`    |
21: | CML 2.9 and older (IOL-L2) | `x86_64_crb_linux-adventerprisek9-ms` | `cisco_iol-L2-17.12.01.bin` | `L2-17.12.01` |
22: 
23: From CML 2.10 the refplat ships IOL as a container image archive; the build pulls the IOL binary
24: out of it for you. Older refplats ship a loose binary under `iol-xe-x.y.z/` (or `ioll2-xe-x.y.z/`),
25: which you must rename yourself — the `.bin` extension is what the build looks for.
26: 
27: Check the result with `docker images`:
28: 
29: ```sh
30: REPOSITORY            TAG           IMAGE ID       CREATED          SIZE
31: vrnetlab/cisco_iol    L2-17.12.01   c207d920446e   5 seconds ago    607MB
32: vrnetlab/cisco_iol    17.12.01      30be6c875c80   12 minutes ago   704MB
33: ```
34: 
35: ### Extracting the binary by hand
36: 
37: Only needed if the automatic extraction breaks. The CML 2.10 archive is a container image archive;
38: the IOL binary sits inside one of its layers.
39: 
40: ```sh
41: # list the layers, in order
42: tar -xzOf iol-xe-17-18-02.tar.gz manifest.json
43: 
44: # list a layer's contents; the binary is a regular file ending in .iol
45: # (binary.iol is only a symlink to it)
46: tar -xzOf iol-xe-17-18-02.tar.gz blobs/sha256/<layer> | tar -tvf -
47: 
48: # pull it out, without unpacking the rest of the archive
49: tar -xzOf iol-xe-17-18-02.tar.gz blobs/sha256/<layer> \
50:   | tar -xOf - x86_64_crb_linux-adventerprisek9-ms.iol > cisco_iol-17.18.02.bin
51: ```
52: 
53: ## Usage
54: 
55: Define the image in a topology. For IOL-L2, add `type: l2`.
56: 
57: ```yaml
58: # topology.clab.yaml
59: name: mylab
60: topology:
61:   nodes:
62:     iol:
63:       kind: cisco_iol
64:       image: vrnetlab/cisco_iol:<tag>
65:       # type: l2  # only for IOL-L2
66: ```

(End of file - total 66 lines)
</content>

Reasoning
So `IOL_PID` is not set anywhere in the repo (it presumably comes from containerlab, which passes IOL_PID env). In the old entrypoint, IOL was exec'd directly and iouyap ran separately in background. Now iouyap-go is the parent that launches IOL. Note new entrypoint drops `-e $num_slots` (ethernet interfaces count) — presumably iouyap-go computes that itself. Also drops the eth0 flush and sleep — presumably iouyap-go handles that. I can't verify iouyap-go's behavior without external access; that's a coverage note.

Potential issues in the Dockerfile:

1. `RUN git clone https://github.com/clab-labs/iouyap-go /src` — clones HEAD of default branch, unpinned. Non-reproducible builds: every docker build may pick up different code. Also no git checksum verification. For supply chain/build reproducibility, this is a P3-ish issue. Many Dockerfiles in the repo may do similar things; check other Dockerfiles for git clone patterns.

2. `FROM --platform=$BUILDPLATFORM ... golang` with `ARG BUILDARCH` and `GOARCH=$BUILDARCH` — this cross-compiles for the target architecture. But `GOAMD64=v1` is passed unconditionally; for ARM64 builds GOAMD64 is ignored (it only applies to amd64). That's harmless. But `BUILDPLATFORM` requires BuildKit — standard now. Also, `--platform=$BUILDPLATFORM` for the golang image requires that BUILDPLATFORM be defined automatically by BuildKit — fine.

Wait, an issue: the build uses `ARG BUILDARCH` — declared, and BuildKit automatically populates BUILDARCH. Fine.

3. Missing `git` in... no, the golang image has git.

4. The `RUN git clone ... /src` then `WORKDIR /src` — fine.

5. `GOARCH=$BUILDARCH` — BUILDARCH is the target platform architecture (e.g., amd64, arm64) which matches Go arch names (amd64, arm64). Good. But if the target is arm/v7 (GOARCH=arm), BUILDARCH would be "arm"? Docker's BUILDARCH values are amd64, arm64, arm, i386, ppc64le, s390x, riscv64. Go arch names match for those. OK.

6. The final image still installs `net-tools`, `curl`, `gnupg`, `ca-certificates`, `sudo` — but the entrypoint no longer uses `ip` (iproute2 was used for `ip addr flush`). Actually the entrypoint no longer calls `ip`. iproute2 still installed — harmless leftover. Not a defect really. Actually the eth0 flush removal: is it needed? The rationale in the old script: "Clear ip addressing on eth0 (it 'belongs' to IOL now)". If iouyap-go now handles that, fine. If not, IOL's management interface config could be broken... I cannot verify. Coverage note / potential P2 uncertainty. Since I can't check iouyap-go, I should put this in coverage notes rather than a finding. But there's a real concern: removal of `ip addr flush` and `sleep 5` changes runtime behavior; if iouyap-go does not do the flush, IOL might behave differently. Uncertain — put in coverage notes as unverified.

7. Dependency: previously iouyap was installed from the gemfury apt repo; the removal also removes the trusted=yes third-party repo — an improvement.

8. `golang:1.26-bookworm` — does that tag exist? Go 1.26... today's date Nov 2026, Go 1.26 would exist (Go 1.26 released ~Feb 2026? Go releases: 1.24 Feb 2025, 1.25 Aug 2025, 1.26 Feb 2026). So plausible. But not verifiable offline. Not a finding.

9. `apt-get clean` but no `rm -rf /var/lib/apt/lists/*` — pre-existing pattern, unchanged. Not relevant.

10. Dockerfile doesn't pin the git clone to a commit/SHA — supply chain + reproducibility finding. Check other Dockerfiles in repo for similar patterns to see if this is a norm.

Let me check other Dockerfiles for git clone usage.

11. The entrypoint: `exec /usr/bin/iouyap 513 -- /iol/iol.bin $IOL_PID -s 0 -c config.txt -n 1024`. Unquoted `$IOL_PID` — if unset, the args shift, iol.bin would get `-s` as its first arg. Previously same issue existed (unquoted $IOL_PID). But now, since iouyap is the direct parent, if IOL_PID is empty, `/iol/iol.bin -s 0 ...` — the -s flag would be interpreted as PID. Pre-existing behavior though; the old script would exec /iol/iol.bin (PID empty → `-e` becomes argv[1]... wait old: `exec /iol/iol.bin $IOL_PID -e $num_slots ...` with empty IOL_PID, the first arg is `-e`. Same failure mode pre-existing. Word splitting: if IOL_PID contains spaces? Unlikely. Not introduced/worsened by this PR. Skip or P4.

12. Missing `-e $num_slots` / `CLAB_IOL_NUM_SLOTS`: The env var was never set by anything in this repo (grep found no other reference). Maybe containerlab sets it? Actually containerlab's cisco_iol kind... I recall containerlab sets `CLAB_IOL_NUM_SLOTS`? I'm not sure. The README of cisco/iol doesn't mention it. Hmm, in containerlab repo, the iol node kind sets env vars like IOL_PID... Let me think: containerlab cisco_iol kind code sets `IOL_PID` env? Actually in containerlab, the IOL node kind computes the number of slots and passes `CLAB_IOL_NUM_SLOTS`. I believe containerlab does set that (in nodes/cisco_iol.go). If iouyap-go now derives the slot count itself (from interface enumeration), removing -e is fine. I can't verify externally. If containerlab still sets CLAB_IOL_NUM_SLOTS, the new entrypoint ignores it — but maybe iouyap-go reads it itself. Unknown. Coverage note.

Now let me examine related code: how the IOL container is launched by vrnetlab.py — the container here uses `ENTRYPOINT /entrypoint.sh`, so vrnetlab isn't used at runtime for IOL (DONT_COPY_COMMON=1, no launch.py). But containerlab calls docker with env IOL_PID etc. Check head vrnetlab.py for iol references — grep showed none in common/. So IOL doesn't use vrnetlab at all; the env variables IOL_PID and CLAB_IOL_NUM_SLOTS are set by containerlab itself (external repo). Since I can't read containerlab source, I should note uncertainty.

Check how containerlab interacts: containerlab's cisco_iol kind — from memory, containerlab sets `IOL_PID` env? Actually in containerlab's `cisco_iol` node, there's code that generates the startup config and sets env vars `IOL_PID`? Hmm. Actually, I recall containerlab sets `CLAB_IOL_NUM_SLOTS`? Not certain. The pre-existing entrypoint used both; the new one uses IOL_PID only. If iouyap-go accepts `-- <cmd> <pid> ...` and spawns iol.bin itself with -e computed from interfaces, that matches the "iouyap-go" design (a rewrite of iouyap in Go that also launches the IOL binary). This is plausible: iouyap-go is a merged tool that acts as both supervisor and UDP relay on port 513, launching the iol.bin with computed eth interfaces.

Given the snapshot cannot confirm iouyap-go CLI semantics, I will flag the unverifiable behavior as coverage notes rather than findings, unless there's clear internal inconsistency.

Internal things I can verify:

A. Dockerfile build reproducibility/pinning: `git clone https://github.com/clab-labs/iouyap-go /src` clones the default branch tip at build time. Unpinned dependency — non-reproducible image builds, and a supply-chain risk (build executes whatever is on the default branch). This is introduced by this PR (base installed iouyap from a third-party apt repo, also unpinned, arguably equally unpinned). Since the base had `deb [trusted=yes]` (worse security-wise), the PR is an improvement. But the new build could still be improved by pinning a ref/commit. Severity: P4 (optional improvement) or P3? Unpinned clone of a third-party repo executed at build time — supply chain. Given base was worse, I'd call it P4.

B. `GOAMD64=v1` unconditional: only relevant for amd64; harmless on arm64. Not a finding.

C. Cross-compilation with `--platform=$BUILDPLATFORM`: correct pattern. But `CGO_ENABLED=0` — fine.

D. `ARG BUILDARCH` declared before use — fine.

E. Multi-arch: GOARCH=$BUILDARCH matches amd64/arm64/arm. OK.

F. Missing `go.mod` go version checks — N/A.

G. The golang builder stage clones the repo every build with no layer caching of dependencies — minor build perf. The clone+build happens every time (no pinned layer). Acceptable.

H. entrypoint.sh removes the `ip addr flush dev eth0` step. If iouyap-go handles flushing, fine; if not, DHCP-assigned address on eth0 (containerlab veth) would remain and possibly conflict with IOL's management interface? Actually IOL's mgmt is eth0 which IOL takes over; the flush was to clear the IP that docker assigned to eth0 so IOL can manage it. Actually wait — in the IOL container, does containerlab set a static IP on eth0 that IOL needs? The flush removed the address so that iouyap/iol can own the interface. If the address remains, IOL may fail to configure its mgmt interface or there may be duplicate address. Since iouyap-go now presumably performs this (the tool's whole point is merging entrypoint+iouyap), I can't confirm. Coverage note as unverified external dependency behavior.

The PR removes an env var handling — if containerlab documented CLAB_IOL_NUM_SLOTS, docs elsewhere might reference it. Grep for NUM_SLOTS found nothing in the snapshot. OK.

J. The `-q` flag dropped from iouyap invocation: old `iouyap 513 -q &`; new `iouyap 513 -- ...`. If iouyap-go supports `-q`, dropping it just means quieter/verbose? Actually `-q` was quiet mode. Unverifiable. Coverage note.

K. entrypoint.sh still installs `iproute2`, `net-tools`, `curl`, `gnupg`, `sudo` — none used by the new 3-line entrypoint. These were in base too; the PR keeps them. `iproute2` was needed by the removed flush commands. Now unused. Minor image size/cleanliness. P4 optional. Actually is `iproute2` used by iol.bin itself or iouyap-go? iouyap-go is Go, presumably uses netlink directly or maybe shells out to ip? Unknown. It's a P4 at best; risk of flagging something actually needed. I'd mention as P4 optional cleanup with caveat? Hmm — "Return only supported, useful findings." The packages were there in base; the PR didn't add them. The PR removed the only in-repo consumer (`ip`). iouyap-go might shell out to `ip` — can't verify. I'll leave as coverage note rather than a finding, or a cautious P4. I think a coverage note is safer.

With `exec`, iouyap becomes PID 1 and must handle SIGTERM/SIGCHLD to propagate stop to iol.bin — containerlab stop relies on docker stop sending SIGTERM to PID1. If iouyap-go handles that, fine. Unverifiable; coverage note.

Assistant · 112026-11-02T12:31:03.186Z
step-start
{
  "type": "step-start",
  "id": "prt_19c3de7590015OjIVuZItcQN11",
  "sessionID": "ses_e63c2efa3ffeOaBxBdTkXhd5wc",
  "messageID": "msg_19c3de012001jSBRswCjQpNy6q"
}
Reasoning
No other Dockerfile in the repo clones from git at build time; this is a new pattern for this repo. So the unpinned clone finding is valid and novel: it's the only place where a build-time `git clone` of an unpinned branch executes third-party code. Compare with base: base installed `iouyap` from a third-party apt repo with `[trusted=yes]` — also unpinned and arguably worse (trusted=yes disables signature checks). So the PR improves supply-chain posture but the unpinned clone remains a reproducibility gap. I'd rate P4 (optional improvement to pin a commit/SHA or ref).

One more thought on the `-e $num_slots` removal and `CLAB_IOL_NUM_SLOTS`: containerlab's cisco_iol kind — I genuinely recall containerlab having code for IOL that sets `CLAB_IOL_NUM_SLOTS`? Hmm. Actually in containerlab repo, `nodes/cisco_iol` sets env vars: `IOL_PID`? Let me think about what the containerlab code does. From containerlab source (cisco_iol node), I remember: it computes `num_slots` based on the number of interfaces and sets `CLAB_IOL_NUM_SLOTS` env var, and `IOL_PID`. Actually I'm fairly sure containerlab sets `CLAB_IOL_NUM_SLOTS` in `nodes/cisco_iol/cisco_iol.go`. Yes — containerlab's cisco_iol.go has something like:

```go
// set the CLAB_IOL_NUM_SLOTS env var
numSlots := (len(n.EthtedInterfaces...)+4)/4
```

Hmm, I genuinely can't be sure. But it's plausible. If containerlab still sets `CLAB_IOL_NUM_SLOTS` and the new entrypoint ignores it, the behavior depends on iouyap-go reading that env var itself. Given the clab-labs/iouyap-go project was created by the same community to replace the C iouyap and launch IOL directly, it likely reads the same env or computes slots from interfaces. I cannot verify without network access. This is exactly the kind of unverifiable external-dependency behavior to put in coverage notes rather than assert as a defect.

However, there is one verifiable-ish correctness concern: the entrypoint no longer computes any fallback. With old entrypoint, `num_slots` was computed from interfaces when CLAB_IOL_NUM_SLOTS unset. New entrypoint passes no `-e` at all. If iouyap-go doesn't infer slot count or read `CLAB_IOL_NUM_SLOTS`, IOL would start with default eth count. But the iouyap-go invocation syntax `iouyap 513 -- <cmd>` shows it takes the guest command — a supervisor designed to spawn the guest and wire its interfaces, so computing eth count from the host's netns is its core job. Reasonable. Unverified → coverage note.

Now, are there any real findings?

1. P4: Unpinned `git clone` of `clab-labs/iouyap-go` in the `iouyap` builder stage — non-reproducible builds; the image contents depend on the default branch tip at build time, and any change to that repo silently flows into published images. Suggest pinning a commit/tag (e.g. `git clone ... && git -C /src checkout <sha>`) or a `GOFLAGS`-style pinned module. Location: cisco/iol/docker/Dockerfile:2.

2. P3/P4: `GOAMD64=v1` — passed unconditionally; only affects amd64 targets. Harmless; too trivial. Skip.

3. P4: leftover packages `iproute2`/`net-tools`/`curl`/`gnupg`/`sudo` now appear unused by the 3-line entrypoint — but the flush removal was deliberate, and iouyap-go may shell out to `ip` (likely, since the Go rewrite of iouyap... actually iouyap-go probably uses netlink or execs `ip`). Too uncertain. Coverage note.

4. Consider whether removing `sleep 5` and the eth0 flush could break: coverage note.

5. Docs: nothing user-visible changed in README; env var `CLAB_IOL_NUM_SLOTS` was never documented in this repo. No doc finding.

6. Schema: not applicable (build scripts, not topology schema).

7. Tests: no tests exist for this path (Makefile smoke tests?). There's no test infra for iol. Verification gap: the PR changes runtime launch behavior (iouyap-go as PID 1 spawning iol.bin); no runnable check exists. Recommend a minimal check? The repo's IOL build requires a proprietary binary; can't test in CI easily. Maybe just note it.

8. Another possible P3: `$BUILDARCH` unset if built with legacy docker builder (DOCKER_BUILDKIT=0) — but `--platform=$BUILDPLATFORM` also requires BuildKit; legacy builds would fail on the FROM line itself. Pre-modern BuildKit is required for multi-stage with --platform; acceptable, no finding.

9. One more subtle thing: `ARG BUILDARCH` is declared after the `RUN git clone` line and before the build RUN. ARG scope: declared at stage level applies from that point. `FROM --platform=$BUILDPLATFORM` uses the automatic BUILDPLATFORM (predeclared), fine. `ARG BUILDARCH` then makes it available. Good.

10. `GOOS=linux` hardcoded — target OS is linux always. Fine.

11. If the user builds with `docker build --platform linux/arm/v7`, BUILDARCH=arm, GOARCH=arm needs GOARM — not set. Minor; IOL binaries are amd64-only anyway (iol.bin is x86 ELF). Realistically amd64/arm64 only. Not worth a finding.

Now, is the unpinned clone worth P4 or P3? The build executes code fetched from a third-party GitHub repo's default branch at image build time. For a repo whose consumers build images locally with their own Cisco binaries, the blast radius is the IOL image. Base was worse (`[trusted=yes]` apt repo + unpinned apt package). I'll rate it P4 — optional but actionable improvement (pin to a tag/SHA).

Anything else? Let me double-check the entrypoint semantics for iouyap-go: `iouyap 513 -- /iol/iol.bin $IOL_PID -s 0 -c config.txt -n 1024`. In original C iouyap, usage was `iouyap <port> [-q]` and it connected UDP port 513 to the IOL process. The Go version's `--` separator passes the guest command. This aligns with iouyap-go's design (I believe iouyap-go takes `-- <command>` to launch the IOL binary). Unverifiable, but internally consistent.

Word-splitting on `$IOL_PID`: unquoted; if `IOL_PID` is unset, `exec /usr/bin/iouyap 513 -- /iol/iol.bin -s 0 -c config.txt -n 1024` — iol.bin would receive `-s` as its first positional arg (the PID slot). iol.bin's first arg is the PID. That would misbehave. But this failure mode existed identically in the base (empty $IOL_PID → `-e` first). Not worsened. Also containerlab always sets IOL_PID. Skip.

Let me also verify there is no CI/test that builds the IOL image (e.g., .gitlab-ci.yml) that might break due to network access requirements. Check .gitlab-ci.yml and .github/workflows/tofarev.yml quickly.

57:

Input

{
  "filePath": "/source/head/makefile-install.include"

Output

<path>/source/head/makefile-install.include</path>
<type>file</type>
<content>
1: #
2: # This Makefile can be included by images that need to run an install phase,
3: # i.e. in addition to doing the docker build, we also want to run some stuff
4: # inside that image to come up with the final output image. in the case of
5: # JUNOS we want to do this as the first time the vMX RE boots up it detects
6: # that it's in a vMX RE mode and then reboots. By starting it up and letting it
7: # do this first check-and-reboot during the image build time we save ourselves
8: # from doing this on *every* run of the container image later.
9: # 
10: # Since we start the virtual router we are actually running a virtual machine
11: # with qemu and for that we want KVM hardware acceleration, which requires
12: # running docker with --privileged. `docker build` doesn't have the
13: # --privileged argument, so instead we first run the build as normal up to the
14: # point where we want to start the virtual router. Then we use `docker run
15: # --privilged ...` do the needful and after commit it using `docker commit ...`
16: # to create the final output image.
17: #
18: # One of the problems with this is that normally the docker build is kind of
19: # idempotent in that it uses a command cache and if there are no changes to the
20: # Dockerfile or input files it will not rerun those commands but use a cached
21: # image. This greatly speeds up the build process. However, when doing this
22: # manual `docker run` dance we miss this opportunity since it will always be
23: # run.... so we worked around it. Before doing docker run we check the SHA sum
24: # of the built image and compare this to the ones of the previously built
25: # image. If they are the same it means the docker build was entirely cached and
26: # there's no need to run the image, otherwise if there's a change we do run it.
27: # When comparing the hashes we omit the last layer of the previous build. It
28: # contains the committed changes from the install phase of the previous build.
29: # Include this makefile to have your image automatically do that dance.
30: 
31: docker-pre-build:
32: 	-cat cidfile | xargs --no-run-if-empty docker rm -f
33: 	-rm cidfile
34: 	-docker tag $(REGISTRY)$(IMG_VENDOR)_$(IMG_NAME):$(VERSION) $(REGISTRY)$(IMG_VENDOR)_$(IMG_NAME):$(VERSION)-previous-build
35: 
36: docker-build: docker-build-common
37: 	-docker inspect --format '{{.RootFS.Layers}}' $(REGISTRY)$(IMG_VENDOR)_$(IMG_NAME):$(VERSION)-previous-build | tr -d '][' | awk '{ $$(NF)=""; print }' > built-image-sha-previous
38: 	docker inspect --format '{{.RootFS.Layers}}' $(REGISTRY)$(IMG_VENDOR)_$(IMG_NAME):$(VERSION) | tr -d '][' > built-image-sha-current
39: 	if [ "$$(cat built-image-sha-previous | sed -e 's/[[:space:]]*$$//')" = "$$(cat built-image-sha-current)" ]; then echo "Previous image is the same as current, retagging!"; \
40: 	docker tag $(REGISTRY)$(IMG_VENDOR)_$(IMG_NAME):$(VERSION)-previous-build $(REGISTRY)$(IMG_VENDOR)_$(IMG_NAME):$(VERSION) || true; \
41: 	else \
42: 	echo "Current build differ from previous, running install!"; \
43: 	docker run --cidfile cidfile --privileged $(REGISTRY)$(IMG_VENDOR)_$(IMG_NAME):$(VERSION) --trace --install; \
44: 	docker commit --change='ENTRYPOINT ["uv", "run", "/launch.py"]' $$(cat cidfile) $(REGISTRY)$(IMG_VENDOR)_$(IMG_NAME):$(VERSION); \
45: 	docker rm -f $$(cat cidfile); \
46: 	fi
47: 	docker rmi -f $(REGISTRY)$(IMG_VENDOR)_$(IMG_NAME):$(VERSION)-previous-build || true
48: 	rm built-image-sha*

(End of file - total 48 lines)
</content>

Reasoning
The Makefile builds with `docker build` (or buildx when DOCKER_PLATFORM set). BUILDPLATFORM is provided by BuildKit automatically for both `docker build` (with BuildKit enabled) and buildx. Fine.

One potential correctness issue: when `DOCKER_PLATFORM` is set (multi-arch builds via buildx), the final stage runs on the target platform... Actually with buildx multi-platform, the final image is built per-platform, and `GOARCH=$BUILDARCH` cross-compiles correctly. Good design.

But there is a subtlety: when `DOCKER_PLATFORM` is set to e.g. `linux/arm64` and build is done on amd64 host with buildx, `--platform=$BUILDPLATFORM` makes the builder stage run as amd64, and GOARCH=arm64 cross-compile works with CGO disabled. Good.

Another subtlety: the RUN `git clone` line — the build stage runs `git clone` every build without cache pinning; combined with unpinned ref, builds aren't reproducible. That's my P4.

Is there anything that would make this a P2/P3? Let me think about whether `golang:1.26-bookworm` exists — as of Nov 2026, Go 1.26 was released around Feb 2026, so the Docker Hub tag exists. Fine.

What about `GOAMD64=v1` — it's the default; harmless. Skip.

Now, the entrypoint change: is there any consumer in this repo that references `CLAB_IOL_NUM_SLOTS` docs? None. Should the removal be flagged? Only if iouyap-go doesn't handle it. Unverifiable → coverage note.

Should I flag the removed `ip addr flush`? Same reasoning → coverage note.

Hmm, but wait — maybe I should double-check the containerlab side assumptions by looking at how the env var was used historically. The base entrypoint computed num_slots locally as fallback. The removal of both the fallback computation AND the -e flag is a deliberate redesign around iouyap-go, which is a supervisor that launches the guest (hence `--`). iouyap-go presumably enumerates interfaces in the container netns and derives the slot count itself (that's precisely why `--` exists — it launches the guest after setting up). So the change is coherent.

One more verifiable point: in the old entrypoint, `exec /usr/bin/iouyap 513 -q &` ran iouyap in the background and then `exec /iol/iol.bin ...` replaced the shell — iouyap became an orphan child of PID 1 (iol.bin). Docker stop sends SIGTERM to iol.bin (PID1), which apparently handles it. In the new entrypoint, iouyap is PID 1 and iol.bin is its child. Stop semantics change: docker stop → SIGTERM to iouyap → iouyap must forward to iol.bin and exit. If iouyap-go does this (as a supervisor, it should), fine. Unverifiable → coverage note. Actually this is the core purpose of iouyap-go per its design; I'll note it.

Also the `sleep 5` removal: with the old entrypoint, there was a 5s delay before iouyap started (to let interfaces appear?). With the new supervisor, iouyap-go presumably waits for the network namespace interfaces itself. Coverage note.

Let me also check whether anything else in the repo (e.g., containerlab node kinds docs in this repo's README.md) mentions iouyap install or the gemfury repo — grep for "gemfury" and "netdevops.fury".

# Code Review: Cisco IOL `iouyap-go` build migration

## Scope

This PR touches exactly two files, both under `cisco/iol/docker/`:

- `cisco/iol/docker/Dockerfile` — replaces the third-party apt package install of `iouyap` (from `netdevops.fury.site` with `[trusted=yes]`) with a multi-stage build that cross-compiles `clab-labs/iouyap-go` from source and copies the binary into the final image.
- `cisco/iol/docker/entrypoint.sh` — collapses the old25-line script (eth0 address flush, `sleep 5`, background `iouyap`, slot-count computation from `$CLAB_IOL_NUM_SLOTS` or interface enumeration, then `exec /iol/iol.bin ...`) into a single supervisor invocation: `exec /usr/bin/iouyap 513 -- /iol/iol.bin $IOL_PID -s 0 -c config.txt -n 1024`.

I traced callers (`cisco/iol/Makefile`, `makefile.include`, `makefile-install.include`, `.gitlab-ci.yml`), docs (`cisco/iol/README.md`), and grepped the whole snapshot for `iouyap`, `IOL_PID`, `CLAB_IOL_NUM_SLOTS`, and `iol.bin` references. The `cisco_iol` kind does not use `common/vrnetlab.py` at runtime (`DONT_COPY_COMMON=1`, no `launch.py` in this image), so the entrypoint is the sole runtime entry point. The `CLAB_IOL_NUM_SLOTS` and `IOL_PID` environment variables are injected by containerlab (external repository), which is not part of this snapshot.

## Summary

| Priority | Count | Summary |
| --- | --- | --- |
| P0 | 0 | None found |
| P1 | 0 | None found |
| P2 | 0 | None found |
| P3 | 0 | None found |
| P4 | 1 | Unpinned `git clone` in the `iouyap` builder stage makes builds non-reproducible |

Overall this is a clean, well-motivated change. It removes a third-party apt repository configured with `[trusted=yes]` (no signature verification) and a prebuilt binary dependency, replacing them with a from-source build using the standard `--platform=$BUILDPLATFORM` cross-compilation pattern with `CGO_ENABLED=0`. The entrypoint simplification is internally consistent with `iouyap-go` acting as a supervisor that launches the guest (`iouyap 513 -- <command>`). No blocking defects were identified in what is verifiable from this snapshot.

## Findings

### P4 — Builder stage clones `clab-labs/iouyap-go` at an unpinned ref

- **Location:** [`cisco/iol/docker/Dockerfile:1-6`](https://github.com/srl-labs/vrnetlab/blob/1b19ec5484a2a544b0f4fdec74cbfa2e5479e34c/cisco/iol/docker/Dockerfile#L1-L6)
- **Problem:** The `iouyap` builder stage runs `RUN git clone https://github.com/clab-labs/iouyap-go /src` and immediately builds the working tree with `go build`. No tag, branch, or commit is pinned, so every image build compiles whatever happens to be the default-branch tip of an external repository at that moment. This is the only `git clone`-at-build-time pattern in the repository (I checked all `Dockerfile`s), so there is no established precedent to defer to.
- **Trigger:** Any rebuild of the `cisco_iol` image, including the CI pipeline in `.gitlab-ci.yml` (`make` → `docker build`), pulls the current state of the external repo. A push to `clab-labs/iouyap-go` changes the resulting `/usr/bin/iouyap` binary without any change to this repository.
- **Impact:** Image builds are not reproducible: two builds of the same commit of this repository can produce different images. Debugging runtime regressions in IOL networking becomes harder because the supervisor binary version is not recorded anywhere. This is an optional improvement rather than a defect — the removed base configuration (`deb [trusted=yes] https://netdevops.fury.site/apt/` with an unpinned `iouyap` package) was strictly worse from a supply-chain standpoint, so this PR is already a net improvement.
- **Correction:** Pin the source: `RUN git clone https://github.com/clab-labs/iouyap-go /src && git -C /src checkout <commit-or-tag>`, with the ref (or better, the commit SHA) declared as an `ARG` with a default, mirroring the versioning the `Makefile` already exposes for the IOL image itself. Optionally record the pinned ref in `cisco/iol/README.md` so users know which `iouyap-go` version ships in the image.

## Coverage notes

- **Incomplete snapshot:** The manifest reports `incomplete: true`; the only omitted files are `cisco/n9kv/docker/OVMF.fd` and `cisco/xrv9k/docker/OVMF.fd` (binary size limit), both unrelated to this PR's two changed paths. The review-rule files referenced by the task (`.cursor/rules/karpathy-guidelines.mdc` and `.cursor/rules/ponytail.mdc`) were unavailable, and `standards.json` is empty, so no repository-specific rules were applied.
- **Source-only review:** I did not build the image or run the container. Everything above is derived from static inspection of the snapshot.
- **External dependency semantics unverified:** The `clab-labs/iouyap-go` repository is not part of this snapshot, so I could not confirm the exact CLI contract of `iouyap 513 -- <command>`. Four runtime behavior changes therefore remain unverified assumptions, consistent with a supervisor rewrite but unconfirmed: (1) whether `iouyap-go` derives the ethernet slot count that the old entrypoint passed via `-e $num_slots` (the `CLAB_IOL_NUM_SLOTS` fallback computation was removed with no in-repo replacement, and containerlab is the likely setter of that variable); (2) whether `iouyap-go` performs the `ip addr flush dev eth0` cleanup that was removed; (3) whether the removed `sleep 5` startup delay is replaced by interface readiness handling inside `iouyap-go`; and (4) whether `iouyap-go` correctly forwards `docker stop`'s `SIGTERM` to the child `iol.bin` now that it is PID 1 instead of `iol.bin`. If any of these do not hold, IOL networking or shutdown behavior would regress; a maintainer with access to the `iouyap-go` source should confirm them.
- **Package set:** The final image still installs `iproute2`, `net-tools`, `curl`, `gnupg`, and `sudo`, while the new three-line entrypoint uses none of them in this repository. `iouyap-go` or `iol.bin` may still shell out to some of them, so I did not raise a finding; a cleanup pass would be reasonable once the external tool's runtime requirements are confirmed.
- **No tests/CI for this path:** The repository has no automated check that builds or smoke-tests the IOL image, so the behavior change (supervisor PID 1, cross-compiled binary) is covered only by manual verification. Given the proprietary image requirements, I did not recommend a new test; confirming the four unverified behaviors above with one manual `clab` deployment of an IOL topology (including a stop/restart cycle) would be the smallest useful validation.
